    window.loadScript = (url, { charset = 'UTF-8', ...attributes } = {}, location = 'head') => {
      const existingScript = document.querySelector('script[src="' + url + '"]');
      if (existingScript) {
        return;
      }

      const script = document.createElement('script');
      script.src = url;
      script.type = 'text/javascript';

      script.async = true;
      script.charset = charset;

      if (attributes) {
        Object.entries(attributes).forEach(([key, value]) => {
          script.setAttribute(key, value);
        });
      }

      if (location === 'head') {
        document.head.appendChild(script);
      } else {
        document.body.appendChild(script);
      }

      return script;
    };

Developers on our platform use a myriad of tools and services from other companies too. Cloudflare provides a rich API for our platform that enables developers to create automations, CI/CD, and integrations that glue together the various parts of their infrastructure. Earlier this month, we announced &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/changelog/post/2026-06-03-public-oauth-clients/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;self-managed OAuth&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, making it easier for customers to create and manage their own OAuth clients for delegated access to the Cloudflare API.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Cloudflare isnât new to OAuth. If youâve used Wrangler, or used integrations from partners like PlanetScale, then youâve already used it. However, until now, third-party OAuth was only available through a small number of manually onboarded integrations, and was not available to developers more broadly. That meant developers building their own integrations had to rely on API tokens, which are harder to manage and a poor fit for many delegated application flows.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Over the last year, we onboarded a growing number of early partners while improving the consent, revocation, and security model behind Cloudflare OAuth. But as our Developer Platform grew and agentic tools drove demand for delegated access, it became clear that opening up OAuth to all customers was critical to the success of our platform.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;With self-managed OAuth, developers can now offer a standard OAuth flow where customers grant scoped access directly, making it easier to build SaaS integrations, internal developer platforms, and agentic tools while giving users clearer consent, easier revocation, and more control over what an application can do.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;scaling-the-ecosystem-securely\&quot;&gt;Scaling the ecosystem securely&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#scaling-the-ecosystem-securely\&quot; to make it clearer which application is requesting access, and what permissions it will receive. We also added revocation to the dashboard so developers can easily control which applications have access to their data, and made app ownership more visible to prevent OAuth phishing attacks.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Opening self-managed OAuth to all customers also required major upgrades to our underlying OAuth engine. This process required a large amount of planning to do with minimal user interruption, while also ensuring data stability and security.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;planning-the-upgrade-to-our-oauth-engine\&quot;&gt;Planning the upgrade to our OAuth engine&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#planning-the-upgrade-to-our-oauth-engine\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Years ago, we deployed &lt;a href=\&quot;https://github.com/ory/hydra\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Hydra&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, an open-source OAuth engine, to power Cloudflare OAuth under the hood. That deployment served us well when usage was limited, but as the developer platform grew and agentic workflows became more common, it became clear that we needed a major upgrade to unlock new capabilities and improve performance.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;As we planned the upgrade, we decided to do two smaller sequential upgrades rather than doing one large upgrade.Â  First, we would move to the latest 1.X release, evaluate any behavior or performance changes, and then proceed with the 2.X upgrade.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;During our upgrade planning, it became clear that even the 1.X upgrade would&lt;i&gt; &lt;/i&gt;still impact customers because the Hydra database required extensive schema migrations that:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ol&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Created indexes in a manner that would claim an exclusive lock on critical tables, preventing active users from performing important OAuth operationsÂ &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Added columns to critical tables, and moved other columns to new tables&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ol&gt;&lt;p&gt;There was also a quirk in the version of Hydra we were using in which the SDK would perform SELECT * operations, causing deserialization issues with the schema changes.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;To prevent user impact, we rewrote the SQL migrations to use features such as CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY, and built a custom version of Hydra which selected explicit columns rather than SELECT *.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;With the latest 1.X upgrade planned out, we now needed to create a plan for the even larger 2.X upgrade. We identified three potential options, and weighed the benefits and drawbacks of each one. Doing an in-place upgrade was not going to work for us, due to the sheer amount of schema changes the major version bump brought with it. We decided that a blue-green strategy would work, but there was more that needed to be done than simply flipping a switch to start using the new version. The upgrade and migration process would take multiple hours, and we needed the system to continue functioning correctly in that time window.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The first blue-green option would involve disabling writes to the database, preventing any new authorizations from occurring. This means they would not be lost in the transition, but it also meant that nobody would be able to use existing OAuth apps unless they already had a valid credential. It also presented another large problem: if users needed to revoke access from an application for any reason, it would not be possible while the upgrade was being performed.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;To combat these issues, we came up with a way to leave writes to the database enabled, at the cost of losing some of them in the switch to the green version. The first thing to solve was minimizing the number of writes for new tokens. There was an operational lever we pulled: increasing the expiry time of tokens to multiple hours. This would allow apps that received new tokens before the upgrade to continue using them without needing to refresh.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;With reducing writes solved, we needed to come up with a way to not lose any revocations our users performed during the upgrade window. To do this, we created a queue system (using &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/queues/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Cloudflare Queues&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;!) which, after a revocation event, would have a record written into the queue with information about that revocation. This would allow us to drain the queue with the database flipped to the green version, replaying all revocation events that took place in the time window in which they would have been lost. This was critical to get right, otherwise applications that users had revoked would inadvertently have their access restored.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;executing-the-upgrade\&quot;&gt;Executing the upgrade&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#executing-the-upgrade\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    \n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;upgrading-to-1-x\&quot;&gt;Upgrading to 1.X&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#upgrading-to-1-x\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;From an operational point of view, our first upgrade to the last 1.X release went off without any hitches. Our custom database migrations ran faster than we expected, with no user impact. We had to do a hard cutover to the new version because the old version was unable to introspect tokens that were created by the newer version.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;After the cutover, we saw an increase in refresh token errors that we had not seen before. This ended up being due to stricter refresh invalidation behaviors in the new version; if a refresh token was reused, Hydra would invalidate the whole access and refresh token chain. This is problematic for Wrangler and MCP clients. These clients both have a high request volume, and a single reused refresh token would invalidate the entire session.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We mitigated this by adding refresh token coalescing behavior to our Worker which routes OAuth traffic to the correct destination. This allowed us to briefly cache the refresh token request before it reached Hydra, so that if we detected a retry we could short-circuit the request and respond without invalidating the tokens. Fortunately, 2.X versions of Hydra have a configurable ârefresh token grace periodâ, which resolves this by allowing a refresh token to be retried for a period of time without invalidating the whole chain.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;upgrading-to-2-x\&quot;&gt;Upgrading to 2.X&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#upgrading-to-2-x\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Since multiple hours of high user-facing impact would not be acceptable, we had our blue-green upgrade strategy set. At a high level, this sounds simple; the migrations would run on a copy of our production database, and then cut over along with the new Hydra version after they complete. In reality, there were a &lt;i&gt;lot &lt;/i&gt;more moving parts:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ul&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Enable revocation replay capture queue&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Copy and restore our database to the new target&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Targeted data cleanup â existing data violated some new constraints introduced in the newer versions, which could prevent migrations from succeeding&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Perform cutovers on the Hydra service along with two additional critical internal systems simultaneously to prevent any errors&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Post-cutover monitoring and validation&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ul&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/tbiQb2wX9zyyC2n6cOMmv/706bacb728d5abef09117c3893ec288d/hydra_upgrade_diagram.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;819\&quot; height=\&quot;692\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;We chose an upgrade window when Hydra had the lowest request volume per second to minimize lost token writes. Other than some timeout tuning, our production migrations ran well against the new database: the net runtime in production was approximately three hours. After the migrations completed, we carefully rolled out the new version of the Hydra service, along with two additional system configs to flip our systems to use the new SDK version.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Shortly after cutting traffic over, we observed that a data cleanup job in our authorization service (which relies on the Hydra consent session API) was being overeager in its purging of OAuth policy data. After investigation, we discovered that there was an issue in one of the Hydra migrations that corrupted the state of certain valid OAuth sessions, which resulted in the migration marking them as invalid. The valid sessions being corrupted caused a disagreement between Hydra and our authorization service, manifesting as an increase in 403s. To mitigate this, we did data restorations and began work on improvements for OAuth authorization behaviors to remove reliance on static policy data.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Beyond the data cleanup issue, there were some additional small fixes more driven by specific client behaviors which we landed quickly.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;With the Hydra version upgrade complete, OAuth traffic has remained stable with improved system performance and reliability for our customers. It also brought production onto the same foundation our newer OAuth APIs had already been validated against in staging, clearing the way for our &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/changelog/post/2026-06-03-public-oauth-clients/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;self-managed OAuth release on June 3&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.Â &lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;performance-improvements\&quot;&gt;Performance improvements&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#performance-improvements\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;After completing a large upgrade like this, it is always rewarding and illuminating to look at some broad metrics about the impact. We gathered additional metrics during the database migrations, and observed considerable performance improvements after the upgrade was complete.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;database\&quot;&gt;Database&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#database\&quot; &lt;th class=\&quot;tg-1wig\&quot;&gt;&lt;span style=\&quot;font-weight:700;font-style:normal;text-decoration:none;color:#000;background-color:transparent\&quot;&gt;Before&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/th&gt;\n app ecosystem with OAuth for all&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;oauth-for-all&quot;],&quot;excerpt&quot;:[0,&quot;Self-Managed OAuth is now available to all developers on Cloudflare. Here&#39;s how we executed a zero-downtime migration of our core OAuth engine to make it happen.&quot;],&quot;featured&quot;:[0,false],&quot;html&quot;:[0,&quot;&lt;p&gt;Cloudflare provides services that help run 20% of the web, but we donât do it alone. Developers on our platform use a myriad of tools and services from other companies too. Cloudflare provides a rich API for our platform that enables developers to create automations, CI/CD, and integrations that glue together the various parts of their infrastructure. Earlier this month, we announced &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/changelog/post/2026-06-03-public-oauth-clients/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;self-managed OAuth&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, making it easier for customers to create and manage their own OAuth clients for delegated access to the Cloudflare API.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Cloudflare isnât new to OAuth. If youâve used Wrangler, or used integrations from partners like PlanetScale, then youâve already used it. However, until now, third-party OAuth was only available through a small number of manually onboarded integrations, and was not available to developers more broadly. That meant developers building their own integrations had to rely on API tokens, which are harder to manage and a poor fit for many delegated application flows.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Over the last year, we onboarded a growing number of early partners while improving the consent, revocation, and security model behind Cloudflare OAuth. But as our Developer Platform grew and agentic tools drove demand for delegated access, it became clear that opening up OAuth to all customers was critical to the success of our platform.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;With self-managed OAuth, developers can now offer a standard OAuth flow where customers grant scoped access directly, making it easier to build SaaS integrations, internal developer platforms, and agentic tools while giving users clearer consent, easier revocation, and more control over what an application can do.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;scaling-the-ecosystem-securely\&quot;&gt;Scaling the ecosystem securely&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#scaling-the-ecosystem-securely\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;While our earlier OAuth solution was sufficient for a small number of carefully managed partners, we realized that our permissions model, our consent experience, and our ways of mitigating potential abuse vectors were not mature enough.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Earlier this year we &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/improved-developer-security/#improving-the-oauth-consent-experience\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;updated our consent experience&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to make it clearer which application is requesting access, and what permissions it will receive. We also added revocation to the dashboard so developers can easily control which applications have access to their data, and made app ownership more visible to prevent OAuth phishing attacks.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Opening self-managed OAuth to all customers also required major upgrades to our underlying OAuth engine. This process required a large amount of planning to do with minimal user interruption, while also ensuring data stability and security.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;planning-the-upgrade-to-our-oauth-engine\&quot;&gt;Planning the upgrade to our OAuth engine&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#planning-the-upgrade-to-our-oauth-engine\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Years ago, we deployed &lt;a href=\&quot;https://github.com/ory/hydra\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Hydra&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, an open-source OAuth engine, to power Cloudflare OAuth under the hood. That deployment served us well when usage was limited, but as the developer platform grew and agentic workflows became more common, it became clear that we needed a major upgrade to unlock new capabilities and improve performance.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;As we planned the upgrade, we decided to do two smaller sequential upgrades rather than doing one large upgrade.Â  First, we would move to the latest 1.X release, evaluate any behavior or performance changes, and then proceed with the 2.X upgrade.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;During our upgrade planning, it became clear that even the 1.X upgrade would&lt;i&gt; &lt;/i&gt;still impact customers because the Hydra database required extensive schema migrations that:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ol&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Created indexes in a manner that would claim an exclusive lock on critical tables, preventing active users from performing important OAuth operationsÂ &lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Added columns to critical tables, and moved other columns to new tables&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ol&gt;&lt;p&gt;There was also a quirk in the version of Hydra we were using in which the SDK would perform SELECT * operations, causing deserialization issues with the schema changes.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;To prevent user impact, we rewrote the SQL migrations to use features such as CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY, and built a custom version of Hydra which selected explicit columns rather than SELECT *.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;With the latest 1.X upgrade planned out, we now needed to create a plan for the even larger 2.X upgrade. We identified three potential options, and weighed the benefits and drawbacks of each one. Doing an in-place upgrade was not going to work for us, due to the sheer amount of schema changes the major version bump brought with it. We decided that a blue-green strategy would work, but there was more that needed to be done than simply flipping a switch to start using the new version. The upgrade and migration process would take multiple hours, and we needed the system to continue functioning correctly in that time window.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The first blue-green option would involve disabling writes to the database, preventing any new authorizations from occurring. This means they would not be lost in the transition, but it also meant that nobody would be able to use existing OAuth apps unless they already had a valid credential. It also presented another large problem: if users needed to revoke access from an application for any reason, it would not be possible while the upgrade was being performed.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;To combat these issues, we came up with a way to leave writes to the database enabled, at the cost of losing some of them in the switch to the green version. The first thing to solve was minimizing the number of writes for new tokens. There was an operational lever we pulled: increasing the expiry time of tokens to multiple hours. This would allow apps that received new tokens before the upgrade to continue using them without needing to refresh.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;With reducing writes solved, we needed to come up with a way to not lose any revocations our users performed during the upgrade window. To do this, we created a queue system (using &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/queues/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Cloudflare Queues&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;!) which, after a revocation event, would have a record written into the queue with information about that revocation. This would allow us to drain the queue with the database flipped to the green version, replaying all revocation events that took place in the time window in which they would have been lost. This was critical to get right, otherwise applications that users had revoked would inadvertently have their access restored.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;executing-the-upgrade\&quot;&gt;Executing the upgrade&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#executing-the-upgrade\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    \n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;upgrading-to-1-x\&quot;&gt;Upgrading to 1.X&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#upgrading-to-1-x\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;From an operational point of view, our first upgrade to the last 1.X release went off without any hitches. Our custom database migrations ran faster than we expected, with no user impact. We had to do a hard cutover to the new version because the old version was unable to introspect tokens that were created by the newer version.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;After the cutover, we saw an increase in refresh token errors that we had not seen before. This ended up being due to stricter refresh invalidation behaviors in the new version; if a refresh token was reused, Hydra would invalidate the whole access and refresh token chain. This is problematic for Wrangler and MCP clients. These clients both have a high request volume, and a single reused refresh token would invalidate the entire session.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We mitigated this by adding refresh token coalescing behavior to our Worker which routes OAuth traffic to the correct destination. This allowed us to briefly cache the refresh token request before it reached Hydra, so that if we detected a retry we could short-circuit the request and respond without invalidating the tokens. Fortunately, 2.X versions of Hydra have a configurable ârefresh token grace periodâ, which resolves this by allowing a refresh token to be retried for a period of time without invalidating the whole chain.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;upgrading-to-2-x\&quot;&gt;Upgrading to 2.X&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#upgrading-to-2-x\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Since multiple hours of high user-facing impact would not be acceptable, we had our blue-green upgrade strategy set. At a high level, this sounds simple; the migrations would run on a copy of our production database, and then cut over along with the new Hydra version after they complete. In reality, there were a &lt;i&gt;lot &lt;/i&gt;more moving parts:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ul&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Enable revocation replay capture queue&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Copy and restore our database to the new target&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Targeted data cleanup â existing data violated some new constraints introduced in the newer versions, which could prevent migrations from succeeding&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Perform cutovers on the Hydra service along with two additional critical internal systems simultaneously to prevent any errors&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Post-cutover monitoring and validation&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ul&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/tbiQb2wX9zyyC2n6cOMmv/706bacb728d5abef09117c3893ec288d/hydra_upgrade_diagram.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;819\&quot; height=\&quot;692\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;We chose an upgrade window when Hydra had the lowest request volume per second to minimize lost token writes. Other than some timeout tuning, our production migrations ran well against the new database: the net runtime in production was approximately three hours. After the migrations completed, we carefully rolled out the new version of the Hydra service, along with two additional system configs to flip our systems to use the new SDK version.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Shortly after cutting traffic over, we observed that a data cleanup job in our authorization service (which relies on the Hydra consent session API) was being overeager in its purging of OAuth policy data. After investigation, we discovered that there was an issue in one of the Hydra migrations that corrupted the state of certain valid OAuth sessions, which resulted in the migration marking them as invalid. The valid sessions being corrupted caused a disagreement between Hydra and our authorization service, manifesting as an increase in 403s. To mitigate this, we did data restorations and began work on improvements for OAuth authorization behaviors to remove reliance on static policy data.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Beyond the data cleanup issue, there were some additional small fixes more driven by specific client behaviors which we landed quickly.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;With the Hydra version upgrade complete, OAuth traffic has remained stable with improved system performance and reliability for our customers. It also brought production onto the same foundation our newer OAuth APIs had already been validated against in staging, clearing the way for our &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/changelog/post/2026-06-03-public-oauth-clients/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;self-managed OAuth release on June 3&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.Â &lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;performance-improvements\&quot;&gt;Performance improvements&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#performance-improvements\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;After completing a large upgrade like this, it is always rewarding and illuminating to look at some broad metrics about the impact. Here&#39;s how we executed a zero-downtime migration of our core OAuth engine to make it happen.&quot;],&quot;imgPreview&quot;:[0,&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/6fEPzehWZ8mfroJDIf30mC/636b651767a7cc41dd8b4e7093490a11/OG_Share_2024-2025-2026__40_.png&quot;]}],&quot;publicly_index&quot;:[0,true]}],&quot;initialReadingTime&quot;:[0,&quot;6&quot;],&quot;relatedPosts&quot;:[1,[[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;6BmERiKIIt4pIJoFmNy7Jn&quot;],&quot;title&quot;:[0,&quot;How we built saga rollbacks for Cloudflare Workflows&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;rollbacks-for-workflows&quot;],&quot;excerpt&quot;:[0,&quot;Cloudflare Workflows, our durable execution engine for multi-step applications, now supports saga-style rollbacks, allowing developers to specify a compensating action for each step.do(). &quot;],&quot;featured&quot;:[0,false],&quot;html&quot;:[0,&quot;&lt;p&gt;Cloudflare Workflows allows you to build durable, multi-step applications with built-in retries and state persistence across long-running processes. When a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/workflows/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Workflow&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; executes, each step can call external systems, retry failures, and persist state across restarts. But if one step fails, it may leave earlier work from completed steps in an inconsistent or partial state.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Today weâre shipping saga rollbacks for Workflows, allowing you to declare rollback logic within the step itself, in case of failure.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;For example, consider a workflow for transferring funds between accounts at two different banks:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ol&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Debit from account at Bank A&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Credit to account at Bank B&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Send email confirmation to both account owners&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ol&gt;&lt;p&gt;What happens if Step 2, the credit to account at Bank B, fails? Once the debit succeeds at Bank A, the transaction is committed and the money has left its system. As the orchestrator of the transaction, you cannot simply âundoâ the operation in Bank A&amp;#39;s system. Instead, the money must be credited back to the account at Bank A through a new operation that semantically reverses the first one.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/1j8xfDeKb3FCgE2Ktxf4fq/723e2b9e34189747d3c8eb65f906fb41/BLOG-3317_image6.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3317 image6\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1376\&quot; height=\&quot;1310\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;\nThis pairing of an operation and its compensation logic is called the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xDuwrtwYHu8\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;saga pattern&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Before today, developers had to implement their own compensation logic to track what succeeded, what failed, and what actions should be taken upon failure, outside of the stepsâ direct definitions. Now, you can define compensation logic for each &lt;code&gt;step.do()&lt;/code&gt; as an argument within the steps themselves, maintaining your workflowâs durability for the rollback as well.&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-TypeScript\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-TypeScript\&quot;&gt;// track what completed so we know what to undo\nlet debitA;\nlet creditB;\ntry {\n  debitA = await step.do(&amp;quot;debit-bank-a&amp;quot;, () =&amp;gt; bankA.debit(from, amount));\n  creditB = await step.do(&amp;quot;credit-bank-b&amp;quot;, () =&amp;gt; bankB.credit(to, amount));\n  await step.do(&amp;quot;notify&amp;quot;, () =&amp;gt; notifyBoth(from, to, amount));\n} catch (error) {\n  // unwind in reverse. each undo is its own durable step,\n  // must be idempotent, and must keep going if one fails.\n  if (creditB) {\n    try {\n      await step.do(&amp;quot;reverse-credit-b&amp;quot;, () =&amp;gt; bankB.debit(to, amount, creditB.id));\n    } catch (e) {\n      await alertOnCall(&amp;quot;reverse-credit-b failed&amp;quot;, e);\n    }\n  }\n  if (debitA) {\n    try {\n      await step.do(&amp;quot;refund-debit-a&amp;quot;, () =&amp;gt; bankA.credit(from, amount, debitA.id));\n    } catch (e) {\n      await alertOnCall(&amp;quot;refund-debit-a failed&amp;quot;, e);\n    }\n  }\n  throw error;\n}&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;&lt;i&gt;&lt;sup&gt;Without rollbacks&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-TypeScript\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-TypeScript\&quot;&gt;// each step ships with its own undo. add a step,\n// add its rollback right here. no growing catch\n// block, no manual ordering, no replay logic.\nawait step.do(&amp;quot;debit-bank-a&amp;quot;, () =&amp;gt; bankA.debit(from, amount), {\n  rollback: async ({ output }) =&amp;gt; bankA.credit(from, amount, output.id),\n});\nawait step.do(&amp;quot;credit-bank-b&amp;quot;, () =&amp;gt; bankB.credit(to, amount), {\n  rollback: async ({ output }) =&amp;gt; bankB.debit(to, amount, output.id),\n});\nawait step.do(&amp;quot;notify&amp;quot;, () =&amp;gt; notifyBoth(from, to, amount));&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;&lt;i&gt;&lt;sup&gt;With rollbacks&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;try-it-out\&quot;&gt;Try it out&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#try-it-out\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;To use rollbacks, just pass an options object containing a &lt;code&gt;rollback&lt;/code&gt; function as the last argument to &lt;code&gt;step.do()&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-TypeScript\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-TypeScript\&quot;&gt;const debit = await step.do(\n  &amp;quot;debit-account-a&amp;quot;,\n  async () =&amp;gt; {\n    return await bankA.debit({\n      accountId: fromAccountId,\n      amount,\n      idempotencyKey: `${transferId}:debit-account-a`,\n    });\n  },\n  {\n    rollback: async () =&amp;gt; {\n      await bankA.credit({\n        accountId: fromAccountId,\n        amount,\n        idempotencyKey: `${transferId}:rollback-debit-account-a`,\n      });\n    },\n  }\n);\n\n// The idempotency keys make both the forward operations and rollback operations safe to retry without duplicating the transfer\n\nconst credit = await step.do(\n  &amp;quot;credit-account-b&amp;quot;,\n  async () =&amp;gt; {\n    return await bankB.credit({\n      accountId: toAccountId,\n      amount,\n      idempotencyKey: `${transferId}:credit-account-b`,\n    });\n  },\n  {\n    rollback: async ({ output }) =&amp;gt; {\n      if (output === undefined) {\n        return;\n      }\n\n      await bankB.debit({\n        accountId: toAccountId,\n        amount,\n        idempotencyKey: `${transferId}:rollback-credit-account-b`,\n      });\n    },\n  }\n);\n\n\n// If we fail here, we may want to revert all previous payments. Users should not have to wrap their code in complex try-catch logic just to revert two small payments (see below)\n\nawait step.do(&amp;quot;send-confirmation&amp;quot;, async () =&amp;gt; {\n  await sendTransferConfirmation({ ... });\n});&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;Rollback functions should be idempotent, just like regular Workflow steps. If you refund a charge, use the payment provider&amp;#39;s idempotency key. If you release inventory, make the release safe to call more than once.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;If any step fails, the rollback handlers will execute in reverse &lt;code&gt;step-start&lt;/code&gt; order. It sounds simple: run the undo steps when something fails. In practice, there are a few details that make the API and execution model important.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;1. &lt;b&gt;The failed step may still need rollback. &lt;/b&gt;A failed &lt;code&gt;step.do()&lt;/code&gt; can still be rollback-eligible if it registered a rollback handler.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The rollback will not start if user code catches an error and the Workflow continues, but if a step error is caught and the Workflow later fails for another reason, rollback can still run for previously registered handlers, which execute in reverse &lt;code&gt;step-start&lt;/code&gt; order.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Why? The step may have partially interacted with an external system before failing. For example, a payment provider may capture a charge, but the step may fail before returning the &lt;code&gt;chargeId&lt;/code&gt; to Workflows. That is why rollback handlers receive &lt;code&gt;output&lt;/code&gt;, but must handle &lt;code&gt;output === undefined&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;2. &lt;b&gt;Rollback only starts when the Workflow fails. &lt;/b&gt;Adding a rollback handler does not mean every step error triggers rollback. If user code catches an error and continues, the Workflow continues. Rollback starts when the Workflow itself is about to fail terminally.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;When rollback starts, Workflows finds eligible &lt;code&gt;step.do()&lt;/code&gt; calls, runs their rollback handlers, then records the final Workflow failure.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;3. &lt;b&gt;Ordering has to be predictable. &lt;/b&gt;For sequential Workflows, rollback order feels obvious:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ol&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Reserve inventory.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Charge card.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Create shipment.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;If shipment fails, refund the card and release the inventory.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ol&gt;&lt;p&gt;Parallel steps make this more subtle. Completion order can differ from start order, so Workflows uses reverse step-start order instead of reverse completion order.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The practical rules are:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ol&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Any started or completed steps with rollback handlers are eligible.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;The failing &lt;code&gt;step.do()&lt;/code&gt; is also eligible if it registered a rollback handler.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Handlers run in reverse step-start order, not completion order.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ol&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;how-we-designed-the-api\&quot;&gt;How we designed the API&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#how-we-designed-the-api\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Once we had the expected behavior in mind, we had to add this new pattern into the Workflows API. Rollbacks went through a few iterations before we landed on &lt;code&gt;rollback options&lt;/code&gt;.Â &lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;why-not-a-fluent-or-builder-api\&quot;&gt;Why not a fluent or builder API?&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#why-not-a-fluent-or-builder-api\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;The first approach was a fluent form: &lt;code&gt;step.do(...).rollback(...)&lt;/code&gt; It reads well. The forward action and the compensation sit next to each other, and the call site looks like ordinary JavaScript chaining.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The problem is that &lt;code&gt;step.do()&lt;/code&gt; already has an important meaning: it starts a durable step and returns a Promise for the step output. In Workers, promise-like values are especially meaningful because Workers RPC supports &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/capnweb-javascript-rpc-library/#chained-calls-promise-pipelining\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;promise pipelining&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, a pattern inherited from systems like &lt;a href=\&quot;https://capnproto.org/rpc.html#time-travel-promise-pipelining\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Cap&amp;#39;n Proto&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Promise pipelining lets code call a method on a future value before that value has fully returned to the caller. For example:&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-JavaScript\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-JavaScript\&quot;&gt;const session = api.authenticate(apiKey);\nconst name = await session.whoami();&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;Here, &lt;code&gt;session&lt;/code&gt; is not the real session object yet. It is more like a handle to the session that will exist soon. When you call &lt;code&gt;session.whoami()&lt;/code&gt;, Workers can send that call to the remote side early and say: âonce authentication creates the session, call &lt;code&gt;whoami()&lt;/code&gt; on it.â&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/cgBccGGKzjrx2gnnyAUvL/f0470a7a40ef05027e952d42abfa592c/BLOG-3317_image4.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3317 image4\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;1332\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;That saves a round trip. The caller does not need to wait for &lt;code&gt;authenticate()&lt;/code&gt; to fully finish before asking for &lt;code&gt;whoami()&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We considered a fluent API:&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-TypeScript\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-TypeScript\&quot;&gt;step.do(&amp;quot;charge-card&amp;quot;, chargeCard).rollback(refundCharge);&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;\nTo a reader, that can look like âcall &lt;code&gt;.rollback()&lt;/code&gt; on the result of &lt;code&gt;charge-card&lt;/code&gt;.â Â  But rollback is not part of the stepâs output. It is part of the &lt;code&gt;step.do()&lt;/code&gt; options, registered before the step starts, so Workflows knows how to compensate the step if a later step fails.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;A fluent API also makes step timing harder to reason about. Today, &lt;code&gt;step.do()&lt;/code&gt; starts the step when it is called, so developers can start a step, do other work, and await the first step later:&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-JavaScript\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-JavaScript\&quot;&gt;const first = step.do(&amp;quot;first&amp;quot;, () =&amp;gt; serviceA.call());\n\nawait step.do(&amp;quot;second&amp;quot;, () =&amp;gt; serviceB.call());\n\nawait first;&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;With todayâs execution model, &lt;code&gt;first&lt;/code&gt; starts immediately, before &lt;code&gt;second&lt;/code&gt;. A fluent API would complicate that. Workflows would need to wait and see whether &lt;code&gt;.rollback()&lt;/code&gt; gets attached before it knows the full step definition. That could delay when the step is sent to the engine.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In the earlier example, &lt;code&gt;first&lt;/code&gt; could start at &lt;code&gt;await first&lt;/code&gt; instead of at &lt;code&gt;step.do(&amp;quot;first&amp;quot;, ...)&lt;/code&gt;, after &lt;code&gt;second&lt;/code&gt; has already completed.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;That makes concurrent Workflows harder to reason about: step timing would depend on when the returned &lt;code&gt;Promise&lt;/code&gt; is consumed, not just where &lt;code&gt;step.do()&lt;/code&gt; is called.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We also considered a builder-style API:&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-TypeScript\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-TypeScript\&quot;&gt;const charge = await step\n\t.saga(&amp;quot;charge&amp;quot;)\n\t.do(() =&amp;gt; chargeCard())\n\t.rollback(() =&amp;gt; refundCharge())\n\t.run();&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;A builder API avoids the &lt;code&gt;Promise&lt;/code&gt; ambiguity. It also gives us an obvious place for future step-level options, and makes it clear that the forward action and rollback action belong to the same saga step.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;But it adds ceremony. Every step needs a final &lt;code&gt;.run()&lt;/code&gt;, forgetting &lt;code&gt;.run()&lt;/code&gt; would be easy and hard to spot without tooling, and simple one-step cases start to look like configuration chains. It also introduces a new &lt;code&gt;step.saga()&lt;/code&gt; builder, breaking from the existing &lt;code&gt;step.&amp;lt;action&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt; pattern. Most importantly, it makes &lt;code&gt;step.do()&lt;/code&gt; feel like an older API rather than the primary Workflows primitive. The goal of rollback was to extend &lt;code&gt;step.do()&lt;/code&gt;, not replace it.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;rollback-as-step-metadata\&quot;&gt;Rollback as step metadata&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#rollback-as-step-metadata\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    \n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-TypeScript\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-TypeScript\&quot;&gt;step.do(..., { rollback })&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;Ultimately, we chose the explicit form where rollback is metadata on the step.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This way, each rollback is defined within the forward step itself. Each handler receives the error that caused the rollback to start, the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/workflows/build/step-context/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;step context&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, and the output, which is either the persisted value returned by the forward step (which can be undefined) or undefined if the step failed before persisting a value.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Rollbacks emit lifecycle events, so you can tell whether compensation started, which rollback handler failed, and whether rollback completed successfully.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Crucially, the original Workflow failure remains separate: rollback is what Workflows does after the failure, not the reason the Workflow failed.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Just as you can define custom retry and timeout behavior in the&lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/workflows/build/workers-api/#workflowstepconfig\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt; &lt;u&gt;step configuration&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; via &lt;code&gt;WorkflowStepConfig&lt;/code&gt;, you add rollback-specific values in &lt;code&gt;rollbackConfig&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-TypeScript\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-TypeScript\&quot;&gt;{\n  rollback: async ({ output }) =&amp;gt; {\n    await bankA.credit({ accountId: fromAccountId, amount, transferId: `${transferId}-reversal` });\n  },\n  rollbackConfig: {\n    retries: { limit: 10, delay: &amp;#039;30 seconds&amp;#039;, backoff: &amp;#039;exponential&amp;#039; },\n    timeout: &amp;#039;2 minutes&amp;#039;,\n  },\n}&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;This matches the lifecycle-event mental model we wanted. A &lt;code&gt;step.do()&lt;/code&gt; already describes a durable unit of work that Workflows records, retries, and later shows in logs. Rollback is another lifecycle behavior for that same unit of work. It should travel with the step definition, not live in a separate wrapper or builder.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ul&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;The step still starts when &lt;code&gt;step.do()&lt;/code&gt; normally starts.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;The returned promise still represents the step output.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Concurrent Workflow code keeps the same execution model.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Retry and timeout options for rollback live next to the rollback handler.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Existing &lt;code&gt;step.do()&lt;/code&gt; calls keep working exactly as they do today.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ul&gt;&lt;p&gt;This shape is slightly more explicit than the fluent API, but that explicitness is useful. The operation and its compensation are still in one place, and the API does not introduce a new step builder or a new kind of promise. Developers who already understand &lt;code&gt;step.do()&lt;/code&gt; only need to learn one additional &lt;code&gt;options&lt;/code&gt; object.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This is less magical, but it is simpler to adopt, and clearer to understand.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;how-it-works-under-the-hood\&quot;&gt;How it works under the hood&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#how-it-works-under-the-hood\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Rollback feels like a small API addition, but it changes what Workflows needs to record about each step.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;A regular &lt;code&gt;step.do()&lt;/code&gt; already has a durable record. Workflows records that the step started, whether it completed, what it returned, and whether it should be skipped instead of repeated if the Workflow resumes later.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Rollbacks add one more thing to that record: whether the step registered compensation logic.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This means Workflows has two pieces of information to bring together if the Workflow fails.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The first is &lt;b&gt;durable step history&lt;/b&gt;. The Workflow engine stores data to know what ran, what completed, what output was saved, and whether rollback was registered.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The second is the &lt;b&gt;rollback handler&lt;/b&gt; itself, which is the function written to compensate for that step. Workflows does not save the text of that function as data. Instead, it keeps a callable reference to the handler while the Workflow is running.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In Workers RPC, this kind of callable reference is called a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/runtime-apis/rpc/lifecycle\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;b&gt;&lt;u&gt;stub&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/b&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. A stub lets one part of the system call code that is running somewhere else. Stubs also have lifetimes such that they can be disposed when a call or execution context ends. If you need to keep a stub past that point, Workers RPC provides a &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/runtime-apis/rpc/lifecycle/#the-dup-method\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;code&gt;&lt;u&gt;dup()&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/a&gt; method, which creates another handle to the same target.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;For rollback, that model is useful. The durable step history records what needs compensation. The rollback stub gives Workflows a way to invoke the compensation code. And because rollback handlers may need to outlive the immediate &lt;code&gt;step.do()&lt;/code&gt; call that registered them, Workflows keeps its own callable reference to the handler for the rollback phase.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In the common case, when a Workflow enters rollback in the same engine lifetime, Workflows already has the rollback stubs it needs. It can use the durable step history to find eligible steps, then invoke the rollback stubs that were registered during forward execution.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This gets more subtle when Workflows has to &lt;b&gt;recover&lt;/b&gt; after a restart.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;If the engine is evicted, crashes, or restarts while rollback is needed, Workflows still has the durable step history, but it may no longer have the in-memory rollback stubs. To recover, Workflows uses &lt;b&gt;replay&lt;/b&gt;: a recovery mode where it can re-run the Workflow code without re-executing completed forward step bodies.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;When replay reaches a completed &lt;code&gt;step.do()&lt;/code&gt;, Workflows reads the persisted result instead of running the step body again. For rollback recovery, Workflows only needs to rebuild handlers for steps that had rollback attached and are eligible for rollback. As those &lt;code&gt;step.do() &lt;/code&gt;calls are encountered, their rollback options can register the callable stubs again&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;That lets Workflows recover the rollback handlers it needs without duplicating the original external side effects.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/6F0SOtk10x2op5YxnKKnXM/54f7e763f4ada07e353e8bcac5549833/BLOG-3317_image5.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3317 image5\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1738\&quot; height=\&quot;1698\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;With those pieces in place, rollback can work whether the handler is still available in memory or has to be rebuilt during recovery.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;When the workflow is about to fail, Workflows does not ask your application to reconstruct what happened. It already has the step history. It can look at the persisted record and answer the important questions:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ul&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Which steps started?&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Which steps finished?&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Which failed step may still need cleanup?&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Which steps registered rollback handlers?&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;What output should each rollback handler receive?&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;What order should compensation run in?&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ul&gt;&lt;p&gt;Then Workflows invokes each rollback stub with a rollback context: the original error, the step context, and the step output, if one was persisted.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The ordering detail matters. In normal JavaScript, especially with &lt;code&gt;Promise.all()&lt;/code&gt;, completion order is not always the same as start order. If step A starts first and step B starts second, step B might finish first. For rollback, Workflows uses the persisted start order as the stable source of truth, then unwinds it in reverse.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Rollback handlers also run through Workflows&amp;#39; normal step machinery. That means compensation gets the same operational properties you expect from Workflows: retries, timeouts, lifecycle events, logs, and a final recorded outcome. If a rollback handler keeps failing after its configured retries, Workflows records the rollback outcome as failed, stops running the remaining rollback handlers, and the Workflow instance ultimately ends in the &lt;code&gt;Errored&lt;/code&gt; state.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This is the main difference between saga rollbacks and a &lt;code&gt;catch&lt;/code&gt; block. A &lt;code&gt;catch&lt;/code&gt; block only knows what is still in memory at its exact point in your JavaScript execution. Workflows rollback uses persisted step history to decide what already happened, invokes the stubs it already has in the common case, and safely rebuilds missing stubs during recovery when it needs to.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;That is also why the API puts rollback on &lt;code&gt;step.do()&lt;/code&gt; itself. Rollback is not a separate global error handler â it is metadata attached to the durable unit of work Workflows already understands.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;whats-next\&quot;&gt;Whatâs next&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#whats-next\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Our first iteration of rollbacks includes:Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;ul&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Explicit per-step rollback handlers for &lt;code&gt;step.do()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Sequential rollback execution&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Retry and timeout configuration for compensation&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ul&gt;&lt;p&gt;Next, we want to explore:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ul&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Rollback support for &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/workflows/build/events-and-parameters/#wait-for-events\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;code&gt;&lt;u&gt;waitForEvent&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Support for parallel rollback execution&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Rollback support for &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/workflows/python/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Python Workflows&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ul&gt;&lt;p&gt;When a multi-step application fails halfway through, the hardest part is often not knowing &lt;i&gt;that&lt;/i&gt; it failed. It is knowing &lt;i&gt;what&lt;/i&gt; already happened, and what needs to happen next.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Saga rollbacks let you put that answer directly beside each step. If you are building multi-step applications with Workflows, try saga rollbacks and tell us what compensation patterns you want next.
