Now itâs time to get to work&quot;],&quot;description&quot;:[0,&quot;The new post-quantum executive order sets a 2030 migration deadline and establishes a powerful foundation for post-quantum resilience. We look at what it gets right, where it can go further, and our migration playbook for government and industry.\n&quot;],&quot;imgPreview&quot;:[0,&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/zFUAcxijyOtuwtQpix988/d03281ce08e06e9281bd252baf29a0ac/BLOG-3360_OG.png&quot;]}],&quot;publicly_index&quot;:[0,true]}],[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;5X4Vz9KDcjIBXgHIFSuJFZ&quot;],&quot;title&quot;:[0,&quot;How we found a bug in the hyper HTTP library&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;hyper-bug&quot;],&quot;excerpt&quot;:[0,&quot;By rearchitecting the Images binding, we accidentally uncovered a bug that existed in the open-source hyper library across multiple major versions.&quot;],&quot;featured&quot;:[0,false],&quot;html&quot;:[0,&quot;&lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/images/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Images&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; service, built in Rust on &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Workers&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, runs on every machine in Cloudflareâs edge network. To handle client connections, we use &lt;a href=\&quot;https://github.com/hyperium/hyper\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;hyper&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, an open-source HTTP library for Rust.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Last year, we &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/improve-your-media-pipelines-with-the-images-binding-for-cloudflare-workers/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;introduced the Images binding&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to enable custom, programmatic workflows for processing remote images in Workers. At the end of 2025, we rearchitected the binding to provide a more direct, local connection between the Workers runtime and the Images service.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Shortly after rollout, we received reports that transformation requests from the binding were failing â but only intermittently and only for larger images. Even stranger, the responses for these requests returned a &lt;code&gt;200&lt;/code&gt; status without any errors logged. The image data was simply cut short: A response that should have been two megabytes might arrive with a few hundred kilobytes instead.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We spent six weeks chasing a nearly invisible bug â a race condition that occurred only under specific conditions â in the hyper library that impacted how the Images binding returned processed image data back to the client. In the end, it took four lines of code to fix it.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;hops-handoffs-and-hyper\&quot;&gt;Hops, handoffs, and hyper&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#hops-handoffs-and-hyper\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;When developers build on Cloudflare, they compose full-stack applications from a set of platform services that are accessible to Workers through bindings. &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/runtime-apis/bindings/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Bindings&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; provide direct APIs to resources on the Developer Platform like &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/products/#compute\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;compute&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/products/#storage\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;storage&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/products/#ai\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;AI inference&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/products/#media\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;media processing&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The Images binding decouples image optimization from delivery; you can transcode, composite, or manipulate images without needing to return the output as an HTTP response. It also lets you apply &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/images/optimization/features/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;optimization parameters&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; in any order, rather than following the fixed sequence imposed by the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/images/optimization/features/#url-interface\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;URL interface&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Here, a worker can pass image data directly to the Images API, chain operations together, and get the processed result back as a stream:&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-JavaScript\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-JavaScript\&quot;&gt;const result = await env.IMAGES\n  .input(image)\n  .transform({ width: 800, rotate: 90 })\n  .output({ format: &amp;quot;image/avif&amp;quot; });\nreturn result.response();&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;At a high level, this is how image data moves through our various services:&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/25KzBWgTy4UfdPbzJOg1lO/9c179230f783e9012624a8a9d99b7dac/image4.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;1118\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;&lt;i&gt;The pipe represents a socket connection between the intermediary and Images, where data is handed off from one process to the next through the kernelâs buffer.&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The binding communicates with Images through a socket connection managed by the Workers runtime. A socket connection is a communication channel between two processes. Each end of the socket has buffers that are managed by the operating systemâs kernel; these buffers are temporary holding areas where data sits after one side writes it but before the other side reads it.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Hyper manages the connection on the Images serviceâs side, reading incoming requests from the socket and writing responses back to it.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;When a request uses the Images binding, the Images service reads the input, performs the requested optimization operations, and encodes the result. It then passes the entire encoded image to hyper as a single in-memory block.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Hyper writes this response data into its own internal buffer. At this point, hyper considers the encoding work as complete, since it has all the bytes that it needs to send. The next step is to flush its internal buffer to the socketâs outbound buffer, moving the data from the Images service to the intermediary on the other end.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;If the reader on the other end is fast, then hyper can flush everything in one pass â the outbound buffer will have room because the reader is consuming data as quickly as it arrives. Once all data is sent, hyper issues a &lt;code&gt;shutdown&lt;/code&gt; on the socket, signaling that the connection is finished and no more data will be written. But if the reader is slower (even by a few milliseconds), then the outbound buffer fills up, and hyper needs to wait until thereâs room to continue writing.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;taking-the-local\&quot;&gt;Taking the local&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#taking-the-local\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;All incoming traffic on Cloudflare&amp;#39;s network passes through FL, an internal intermediary service that runs security and performance features and routes requests to the appropriate backend. When we first launched the binding, image data flowed from the Workers runtime, through FL, to the Images service.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This path was a natural fit for our initial release and follows the same architecture as our URL interface. Over time, though, this coupling with FL became a constraint: Every change to the binding had to follow FLâs release cycle.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In December 2025, the Images team replaced FL with a new intermediary service, an internal worker binding that runs on the same machine. In the original architecture, data moved through FL over network sockets; this path carried the overhead of FLâs full processing pipeline, such as DNS lookups and routing.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The internal binding replaced these with Unix sockets to directly connect the services on the same machine, bypassing FL and the overhead of the network stack. This made the request path to Images faster and gave the team independent control over binding releases.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Within days of the rollout, we received our first customer report.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;200-ok-not-ok\&quot;&gt;200 OK (not OK)&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#200-ok-not-ok\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;The first sign of trouble came from a customer with a non-standard setup: two layers of image processing, where one pipeline was nested inside another.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;First, their worker used the Images binding to composite multiple large source images from R2 â a JPEG background plus PNG overlay layers â into a single combined JPEG. Second, they further compressed, transcoded, and resized the result through the URL interface.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/2K8AiqgOV7Ka7uUUAnk5x0/ca1cc575e08b103ebb677ef66df7e87b/image2.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;1118\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;&lt;i&gt;The bug originated in the inner pipelineâs return path, where the response was truncated before reaching the outer pipeline.&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The inner pipeline (transformation binding) handled compositing. The outer pipeline (transformation URL) handled delivery optimizations like scaling and format conversion. This layered approach meant that when the inner pipeline silently returned a truncated response, the only visible error appeared one level up:&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-Rust\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-Rust\&quot;&gt;error reading a body from connection: end of file before message length reached&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;The outer pipeline received HTTP &lt;code&gt;200&lt;/code&gt; from the inner one, with a &lt;code&gt;Content-Length&lt;/code&gt; header that promised several megabytes. The actual body was only a fraction of that: In one request, only ~200 KB arrived out of an expected 3.3 MB. The error surfaced in the outer pipeline, but the truncation could have originated in the binding, the intermediary service, the Images service, or somewhere in between.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;When a browser receives a truncated image, the result is visible. Depending on the format, the image either renders partially (e.g., with the bottom half missing or gray) or fails to decode entirely, instead displaying a broken image.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;debugging-in-the-dark\&quot;&gt;Debugging in the dark&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#debugging-in-the-dark\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;From here, we worked inward through the request path, testing each layer to isolate where the truncation was happening. Some of these efforts hit dead ends; others left breadcrumbs that narrowed the search:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ul&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Building a reproduction.&lt;/b&gt; We built a worker that mimicked the customerâs nested setup, then stripped away layers until we could trigger the bug with the binding alone. A small script let us fire requests in batches. In one early run, 19 out of 25 requests failed. The amount of data that did arrive â roughly 200 KB â was suspiciously close to the size of the socket buffer in production. This confirmed that the problem wasnât tied to the customerâs configuration and gave us a reliable way to trigger the bug on demand.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Investigating timeouts.&lt;/b&gt; Early on, we suspected the truncation might be related to timeout behavior (i.e., the connection was being closed after a time limit). This theory didnât hold, as the truncation wasnât correlated with request duration.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Updating hyper version.&lt;/b&gt; When the bug was first reported, we were running 0.14.x, while the latest hyper version was around 1.8.x. We tested across hyper versions 0.14, 1.7, and 1.8, just in case the most obvious answer was the correct (and easiest) one. But the bug appeared in each version, which meant that there wasnât an upstream fix.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Reproducing locally.&lt;/b&gt; We ran local integration tests on macOS and a Debian VM. Even under considerable load, our local requests never triggered any failure. Making direct curl requests to the binding socket and replaying captured requests always seemed to work. The bug only appeared on the full production path when there was real concurrency and a real Workers runtime client on the other end of the socket. This led us to suspect the runtime itself.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Ruling out the Workers runtime.&lt;/b&gt; We examined the HTTP client that the Workers runtime uses to communicate with Images through the binding socket. None of the traces from either side of the connection showed any syscalls that indicated an unexpected close or early termination. We observed that the client behaved correctly and multiple other services used the same client without issues.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Distributed tracing.&lt;/b&gt; By inspecting request traces end-to-end, we confirmed that the truncated body was already present before it reached the outer transformation layer in the customerâs setup. That narrowed the problem to the inner pipeline â the binding path through the Images service.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Instrumenting the intermediary service.&lt;/b&gt; We added instrumentation to the intermediary service to measure body sizes before forwarding the response data. The bodies were already truncated by the time they left the Images service, so the intermediary was ruled out.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Deeper tracing within the Images service.&lt;/b&gt; At the service level, the request was processed, the image was properly encoded, and the response was sent with HTTP &lt;code&gt;200&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ul&gt;&lt;p&gt;The only consistent signal was that the bug was timing-dependent: It appeared only on the production path, with real concurrency, and only for larger images.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;a-kernel-of-truth\&quot;&gt;A kernel of truth&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#a-kernel-of-truth\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Tools for application-level debugging told only what the system thought it was doing. But according to the system, everything was fine: Tracing said the response was sent; logging reported no errors, and the Images service returned &lt;code&gt;200&lt;/code&gt; on every request.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;To see what the system was actually doing, we attached &lt;code&gt;strace&lt;/code&gt; to the Images service. &lt;code&gt;strace&lt;/code&gt; records the syscalls that a process makes to the kernel, which could show us exactly which bytes were written, when a shutdown was called, and whether the client sent any termination signal.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Setting up the trace was delicate. &lt;code&gt;strace&lt;/code&gt; works by intercepting syscalls as they happen, which adds a small amount of timing overhead to each one. Filtering for a narrow set of syscalls kept that overhead minimal. Broadening the filter, however, slowed the process just enough to shift the timing between the flush and the shutdown check â and make the bug disappear entirely. That alone reinforced our theory that the issue was timing-sensitive.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Using a reproduction worker, we triggered the bug and compared the syscall output between successful and failing requests.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In a successful request, the response is written in chunks as the socket buffer allows, with shutdown called only after all the data is sent. For example, this may look like:&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-Shell\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-Shell\&quot;&gt;sendto(42, &amp;quot;HTTP/1.1 200 OK\\r\\nContent-Length: 14991808\\r\\n...&amp;quot;, ...) = 219264\nsendto(42, &amp;quot;\\xff\\xd8\\xff\\xe0...&amp;quot;, 292352) = 292352\n// ... keeps writing until buffer drains ...\nsendto(42, &amp;quot;...&amp;quot;, 292352) = 292352\nshutdown(42, SHUT_WR) = 0&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;When we reproduced the bug, a failing request looked like:&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-Shell\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-Shell\&quot;&gt;sendto(42, &amp;quot;HTTP/1.1 200 OK\\r\\nContent-Length: 14991808\\r\\n...&amp;quot;, ...) = 219264\nshutdown(42, SHUT_WR) = 0&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;Here, there is only one write â just enough for the headers and a sliver of the body â before the shutdown is immediately called. Out of a 14.9 MB response, only about 219 KB was sent. The remaining ~14.8 MB of image data never left hyperâs internal buffer, nor was there any termination signal from the client between the write and the shutdown. Instead, the Images service prematurely shut down the connection on its own, genuinely believing it was finished.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The failing requests confirmed that the bug was a race condition that triggered intermittently. Whether a request succeeded or failed depended on whether the flush and shutdown operations overlapped, which changed from request to request. When the buffer was still full at the exact moment that hyper decided the connection was finished, data was lost.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/7IaYVui9xakhcroOBifwmO/c22c004bcb85c7efe38f318b9db92a3f/image3.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;1118\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;&lt;i&gt;When the reader consumes slower than hyper writes, the outbound buffer fills up. If hyper shuts down the connection before the buffer drains, then only a fraction of the response makes it to the intermediary; this incomplete data gets forwarded back to the Workers runtime and the client.&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The December rearchitecture didn&amp;#39;t introduce this bug, which had been present in hyper for years across multiple major versions. But the new intermediary changed who was reading on the response side of the socket. Our working theory is that FL, the previous intermediary, consumed data fast enough that the socket buffer rarely filled during a response. The new reader read at a pace that occasionally let the buffer fill during larger responses.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;These few milliseconds of backpressure, introduced by an improvement that made everything else faster, were all it took to surface a flaw that had been hiding in plain sight.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;inside-the-dispatch-loop\&quot;&gt;Inside the dispatch loop&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#inside-the-dispatch-loop\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Hyper&amp;#39;s HTTP/1 connection lifecycle is driven by a state machine in a file called &lt;code&gt;dispatch.rs&lt;/code&gt;. It runs a loop that reads requests, writes responses, flushes the write buffer to the socket, and decides when to shut down. In simplified form:&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-Rust\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-Rust\&quot;&gt;fn poll_loop(&amp;amp;mut self, cx: &amp;amp;mut Context&amp;lt;&amp;#039;_&amp;gt;) -&amp;gt; Poll&amp;lt;Result&amp;lt;(), Error&amp;gt;&amp;gt; {\n    loop {\n        let _ = self.poll_read(cx)?;\n        let _ = self.poll_write(cx)?;\n        let _ = self.poll_flush(cx)?;\n\n        if !self.conn.wants_read_again() {\n            return Poll::Ready(Ok(()));\n        }\n    }\n}&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;More precisely, the &lt;code&gt;let _&lt;/code&gt; before &lt;code&gt;poll_flush&lt;/code&gt; is where the bug lives.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In Rust, &lt;code&gt;let _ = expr&lt;/code&gt; discards the expression&amp;#39;s result, including &lt;code&gt;Poll::Pending&lt;/code&gt;, the signal that the flush isnât done yet. The flush might still have megabytes sitting in its buffer, but the loop never finds out.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;When a request fails, this is the exact sequence of events:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ol&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;The Images service finishes encoding the image and hands the entire response to hyper as a single in-memory block.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Hyper writes the block into its internal buffer and marks its write state as &lt;code&gt;Writing::Closed&lt;/code&gt;. From an encoding standpoint, the work is done â there is nothing left to encode.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Hyper calls &lt;code&gt;poll_flush&lt;/code&gt; to move the buffered data to the socket. In our previous example, the socket accepted about 219 KB. The remaining ~14.8 MB stays in hyper&amp;#39;s buffer. The socket is full, so the kernel returns &lt;code&gt;Poll::Pending&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;poll_loop&lt;/code&gt; discards the &lt;code&gt;Poll::Pending&lt;/code&gt; with &lt;code&gt;let _&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;It checks &lt;code&gt;wants_read_again()&lt;/code&gt;. The full request was already received, so this returns &lt;code&gt;false&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;poll_loop&lt;/code&gt; returns &lt;code&gt;Poll::Ready(Ok(()))&lt;/code&gt;, signaling that the loop is finished, even though the flush is not.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;poll_shutdown()&lt;/code&gt; fires. The &lt;code&gt;SHUT_WR&lt;/code&gt; syscall is issued.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;The client receives 219 KB and an EOF (end-of-file) indicating that the connection is closed, even though it expects 14.9 MB.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ol&gt;&lt;p&gt;In the second step, hyper marks the write operation as complete as soon as the response body is buffered (i.e., when encoding is finished), rather than when it has actually been flushed. Most of the time, the flush completes in a single pass and this distinction is invisible. On the rare occasions when the socket buffer is full, the flush has to wait â even though hyper doesn&amp;#39;t. The bytes are still sitting in hyperâs buffer, waiting to be flushed to the socket. Hyper proceeds to shut down the connection with this data still in the buffer.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This also explains why curl never triggered the bug. Curl reads data as fast as it arrives: The socket buffer never fills, the flush always completes immediately, and the discarded return value is harmless. The production path, with a reader that occasionally paused for a few milliseconds, was the only configuration where the buffer filled at exactly the wrong moment.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;dont-forget-to-flush\&quot;&gt;Donât forget to flush&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#dont-forget-to-flush\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;After weeks of investigation, the fix itself was conceptually simple. Hyper needed to check whether the flush was actually done before moving on.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Our reproduction worker confirmed that the bug existed, but it couldn&amp;#39;t tell us why a given request failed. Before writing the fix, we needed a test that could trigger the exact socket conditions inside hyper.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We knew the conditions that triggered the bug: a socket that accepts one chunk of data and then blocks. To test with a controlled scenario, we built a custom wrapper around a TCP stream that simulated a full socket buffer. The wrapper accepted 8 KB on the first write, then returned &lt;code&gt;Poll::Pending&lt;/code&gt; on every subsequent write, mimicking a reader that stopped draining the buffer.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The test sent a 500 KB response through this constrained socket and checked whether hyper called shutdown while 492 KB was still buffered. Without a fix, it did. With the fix, it waited.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Initially, we applied the fix in hyperâs dispatch loop. Instead of discarding the result of &lt;code&gt;poll_flush&lt;/code&gt;, we checked to see whether the flush was actually done:&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-Rust\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-Rust\&quot;&gt;let flush_result = self.poll_flush(cx)?;\n\nif flush_result.is_pending() {\n    return Poll::Pending;\n}\n\nif !self.conn.wants_read_again() {\n    return Poll::Ready(Ok(()));\n}&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;If the flush hasn&amp;#39;t completed, then the loop returns &lt;code&gt;Poll::Pending&lt;/code&gt; to the asynchronous runtime. The runtime waits for the socket to become writable, then wakes the task back up to continue the flush. The connection shuts down only after all data has been sent.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;When we deployed this fix, we observed that every byte was written and the shutdown was called only after the buffer was actually empty. The customer who made the first report also confirmed that the issue disappeared.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;While our initial solution worked, the dispatch loop wasnât the right place for the fix. Returning &lt;code&gt;Poll::Pending&lt;/code&gt; early could slow down other operations on the same connection by reducing how frequently reads are polled, causing unintended backpressure. It also doesn&amp;#39;t correctly handle keepalive connections, where a single connection handles multiple requests in sequence â these should remain reusable even while the previous response is still being flushed. Neither issue affected our particular service (where keepalive is disabled), but both could affect other hyper users if the fix were contributed upstream.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We traced through hyper&amp;#39;s connection lifecycle and found a more targeted approach. Rather than changing how the dispatch loop behaves, we applied the fix at the point where shutdown is actually called. Before shutting down the socket, hyper should first flush any remaining data in its buffer:&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-Rust\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-Rust\&quot;&gt;pub(crate) fn poll_shutdown(\n    &amp;amp;mut self,\n    cx: &amp;amp;mut Context&amp;lt;&amp;#039;_&amp;gt;,\n) -&amp;gt; Poll&amp;lt;io::Result&amp;lt;()&amp;gt;&amp;gt; {\n    ready!(self.poll_flush(cx)?);\n    Pin::new(&amp;amp;mut self.io).poll_shutdown(cx)\n}&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;This leaves the dispatch loop unchanged. It adds a flush only at the exact point where data loss would otherwise occur â the moment before shutdown.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;what-stayed-with-us\&quot;&gt;What stayed with us&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#what-stayed-with-us\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;None of the tools at the application level surfaced any errors, crashes, or log entries that provided useful clues. Application-level observability can have a blind spot for bugs that live below its awareness.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The failure occurred intermittently, scaled with response size, couldnât be reproduced with simple tools like curl, and disappeared when we observed the system more closely. These signals pointed to a timing-dependent bug in the connection layer, not in the application logic.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Our breakthrough came from using kernel-level tooling with &lt;code&gt;strace&lt;/code&gt;, the one layer that records what actually happened on the socket. The underlying bug lived in the few milliseconds between a partial flush and a premature shutdown â a window that opened only after we made the system faster.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We merged our fix and the deterministic test into &lt;code&gt;hyperium/hyper&lt;/code&gt; via &lt;a href=\&quot;https://github.com/hyperium/hyper/pull/4018\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;PR #4018&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. It will be available in a future hyper release, ensuring that any service using hyperâs HTTP/1 implementation wonât lose response data to the same race condition.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In the meantime, weâre running an internal fork with the patch applied. This fix stabilized the bindingâs architecture, creating a reliable foundation to expand its functionality.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The Images binding initially covered only transformations of remote images. Earlier this month, we announced that &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/changelog/post/2026-06-10-hosted-images-binding/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;the Images binding now supports operations for hosted images&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, giving developers a unified way to build media-rich applications on Cloudflare.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Read more about how the binding works in &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/images/storage/binding/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;our library&quot;],&quot;description&quot;:[0,&quot;By rearchitecting the Images binding, we accidentally uncovered a bug that existed in the open-source hyper library across multiple major versions.&quot;],&quot;imgPreview&quot;:[0,&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/2QsMQWQPZ5tqju5AHwfCL1/803907dd72b265d4be0b753ec5ef495d/BLOG-3318_OG.png&quot;]}],&quot;publicly_index&quot;:[0,true]}],[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;2EKaG8rvH4FLnpEQVgvJ4I&quot;],&quot;title&quot;:[0,&quot;Temporary Cloudflare Accounts for AI agents&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;temporary-accounts&quot;],&quot;excerpt&quot;:[0,&quot;The moment an agent needs to deploy something, it slams face-first into a wall built for humans. Today we&#39;re rolling out Temporary Accounts on Cloudflare Workers. Any agent can now run wrangler deploy â temporary and get a live Worker in seconds.&quot;],&quot;featured&quot;:[0,false],&quot;html&quot;:[0,&quot;&lt;p&gt;Everyone&amp;#39;s writing code with AI agents today. But the moment an agent needs to deploy something â and needs to sign up and create an account â it slams face-first into a wall built for humans: a browser-based OAuth flow, a dashboard to click through, an API token to copy-paste, a multi-factor authentication prompt to satisfy. For an interactive copilot sitting next to a developer, that&amp;#39;s annoying. For a background agent, it&amp;#39;s a hard stop.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Today we&amp;#39;re rolling out Temporary Cloudflare Accounts for Agents.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Agents can now deploy &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/solutions/frontends/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;websites&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/products/workers/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;APIs&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/products/agents/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;agents&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; right away, without first needing to sign up for an account.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/6joSXmAJAARgyGphPhXGla/9c3e8cef6f269df6a9c458495c5fe40b/image2.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;442\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;Any agent can now run &lt;code&gt;wrangler deploy --temporary &lt;/code&gt;and deploy a Worker to Cloudflare. This temporary deployment stays live for 60 minutes, during which time you can claim the temporary account, making it permanently your own. If you don&amp;#39;t, it expires on its own.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/7IKqILTXCvDH4QjNFtx2pp/798c00b190c9b9909a4501f29b8c18bb/image4.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;694\&quot; height=\&quot;369\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;Our goal? Let your agent code and ship.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;why-frictionless-deployments-matter-for-ai-agents\&quot;&gt;Why frictionless deployments matter for AI agents&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#why-frictionless-deployments-matter-for-ai-agents\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Frictionless temporary accounts matter more than it might first seem:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ul&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Background AI sessions have no human in the loop, and are becoming the norm&lt;/b&gt;. Any auth step that needs a browser, a copy-paste, or &amp;quot;click here in 60 seconds&amp;quot; means an agent gets stuck and may choose to deploy elsewhere.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Trial-and-error is the agent&amp;#39;s superpower&lt;/b&gt;. Agents need a tight write â deploy â verify loop. They need cheap, throwaway deployment targets, so they can curl their own output and decide whether they got it right.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Agent platforms are building their own ways for deploying code to &amp;quot;just work&amp;quot; without extra steps or credentials&lt;/b&gt;. People are starting to expect that this process just works, without the need to sign up for other services that they&amp;#39;ve not used before or heard of.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ul&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;how-it-works\&quot;&gt;How it works&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#how-it-works\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Temporary accounts are built around &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/wrangler/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Wrangler&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, our Developer Platform command-line interface (CLI) tool that lets developers bootstrap new projects, manage their configurations and resources, and deploy and update them.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Wrangler usage is widely &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/examples/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;documented&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; online and agents know how to use it very well. But if you hadnât yet signed in and granted Wrangler permission to your Cloudflare account, when the agent tried to deploy, it would get stuck at the sign-up and authentication step. And you might rightly ask: How do agents and LLMs know that this new &lt;code&gt;--temporary&lt;/code&gt; flag in Wrangler exists, so that they actually use it without a human explicitly telling them to do so?&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;To solve this, we updated Wrangler to prompt the agent with a message that tells it about the &lt;code&gt;--temporary&lt;/code&gt; flag:&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/4pF05TQ9S8HXvOmwD1zoG0/d99ae0ec7d82f26f85dbd5957759697e/image3.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;669\&quot; height=\&quot;184\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;When the agent discovers this, and then runs &lt;code&gt;&lt;b&gt;wrangler deploy&lt;/b&gt;&lt;/code&gt; again with the &lt;code&gt;--temporary&lt;/code&gt; flag, Cloudflare provisions a temporary account for the agent to use, gives Wrangler an API token to work with, and provides a claim URL that the agent can give back to the human.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;lets-go-over-every-step-of-the-flow\&quot;&gt;Letâs go over every step of the flow&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#lets-go-over-every-step-of-the-flow\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Wrangler release&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, fire up your favorite coding agent, and write a prompt to deploy a &amp;quot;hello world&amp;quot; app in build mode:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;blockquote&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;Make a very simple hello world Cloudflare Worker in TypeScript and deploy it using wrangler, don&amp;#39;t ask me questions, do the best you can&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/blockquote&gt;&lt;p&gt;The agent will run wrangler, pick up the &lt;code&gt;--temporary&lt;/code&gt; flag from the output messages, build your script, and deploy it instantly, no human in the loop required:&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/1Okmqkf7nBH7Hu2wsKWmnc/879cb6025e630bbd8a99b546de935828/image7.png\&quot; flag, curled the preview link it got from the output, and verified that the result matches the code.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This is great, but agentic coding is often not about one single deployment. A session can go through a cycle of multiple code changes. This is not a problem: the agent can iterate on the Worker script and redeploy the changes as many times as it wants (within the 60-minute claim window). Type this prompt:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;blockquote&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;Now change hello world to &amp;quot;hello cloudflare&amp;quot; and redeploy&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/blockquote&gt;&lt;p&gt;Look at the agent changing the source code, reusing the previously created temporary account, redeploying a new version and rechecking the result:&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/3W86UmPyW5oQxpGkmEEJzL/6168e21df00111aedab481fee5b0a313/image5.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;727\&quot; height=\&quot;520\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;claiming-the-account\&quot;&gt;Claiming the account&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#claiming-the-account\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;At any point, you can claim the temporary account and make it yours permanently. When you click the claim link you will be taken to a page where you can either sign up for or sign in to Cloudflare, and then claim the temporary account that your Worker was deployed to. This includes claiming not just Workers, but resources like databases and other bindings, too.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/3rrCw7HiJJxy6k3eyipWLR/5aca8cdcb2fc391ea27bab5f335449b0/image6.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1654\&quot; height=\&quot;1200\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;If you do not claim these temporary accounts within 60 minutes, they will be automatically deleted.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;the-road-to-frictionless-agentic-deployments\&quot;&gt;The road to frictionless agentic deploymentsÂ &lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-road-to-frictionless-agentic-deployments\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;This is just one way weâre eliminating the signup barrier for agents. We recently &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/agents-stripe-projects/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;announced a partnership with Stripe&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; and a new protocol we co-designed that lets agents provision Cloudflare on behalf of their users â creating an account, starting a subscription, registering a domain, and getting an API token to deploy code, with no copy-pasting tokens or entering credit card details. Last month, we collaborated with WorkOS on the launch of &lt;a href=\&quot;https://workos.com/auth-md\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;auth.md&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, which anyone can adopt, to let agents provision new accounts using well-established, existing OAuth standards.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Thereâs a ton going on in this space, and weâre excited to keep making it easier for agents to use Cloudflare, and for developers to make their own apps &lt;a href=\&quot;https://isitagentready.com/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;agent-ready&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. Temporary accounts are one more step toward frictionless agentic deployments â stay tuned for more.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Temporary accounts have some limitations, and their capabilities may change over time; check the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/platform/claim-deployments/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;developer documentation&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; for more information and then go build something. ph3 ph0-l fs-20px"><h1 class="f6 f7-l fw4 gray1 pt1 pt3-l mb1">Unlocking the Cloudflare app ecosystem with OAuth for all</h1><p class="f3 fw5 gray5 db di-l mt2">2026-06-24</p><ul class="author-lists class="post-full-content"><div class="mb2 gray5">6 min read</div><img class="mr2" src="https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/470YV3u8pJdiSjyhZl1Zro/12aaff16d7787082571eeee0d2b8217f/image1.png" alt=""/><div class="post-content post-body-content lh-copy gray1"><p>Cloudflare provides services that help run 20% of the web, but we donât do it alone. Developers on our platform use a myriad of tools and services from other companies too. Cloudflare provides a rich API for our platform that enables developers to create automations, CI/CD, and integrations that glue together the various parts of their infrastructure. Earlier this month, we announced <a href="https://developers.cloudflare.com/changelog/post/2026-06-03-public-oauth-clients/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"><u>self-managed OAuth</u></a>, making it easier for customers to create and manage their own OAuth clients for delegated access to the Cloudflare API.</p><p>Cloudflare isnât new to OAuth. If youâve used Wrangler, or used integrations from partners like PlanetScale, then youâve already used it. However, until now, third-party OAuth was only available through a small number of manually onboarded integrations, and was not available to developers more broadly. That meant developers building their own integrations had to rely on API tokens, which are harder to manage and a poor fit for many delegated application flows.Â </p><p>Over the last year, we onboarded a growing number of early partners while improving the consent, revocation, and security model behind Cloudflare OAuth. But as our Developer Platform grew and agentic tools drove demand for delegated access, it became clear that opening up OAuth to all customers was critical to the success of our platform.Â </p><p>With self-managed OAuth, developers can now offer a standard OAuth flow where customers grant scoped access directly, making it easier to build SaaS integrations, internal developer platforms, and agentic tools while giving users clearer consent, easier revocation, and more control over what an application can do.</p>
    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h2 id="scaling-the-ecosystem-securely">Scaling the ecosystem securely</h2>
      <a href="#scaling-the-ecosystem-securely" aria-hidden="true" class="relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5">
        <svg width="16" height="16" viewBox="0 0 24 24"><path fill="currentcolor" d="m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z"></path></svg>
      </a>
    </div>
    <p>While our earlier OAuth solution was sufficient for a small number of carefully managed partners, we realized that our permissions model, our consent experience, and our ways of mitigating potential abuse vectors were not mature enough.Â </p><p>Earlier this year we <a href="https://blog.cloudflare.com/improved-developer-security/#improving-the-oauth-consent-experience"><u>updated our consent experience</u></a> to make it clearer which application is requesting access, and what permissions it will receive. We also added revocation to the dashboard so developers can easily control which applications have access to their data, and made app ownership more visible to prevent OAuth phishing attacks.Â </p><p>Opening self-managed OAuth to all customers also required major upgrades to our underlying OAuth engine. This process required a large amount of planning to do with minimal user interruption, while also ensuring data stability and security.</p>
    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h2 id="planning-the-upgrade-to-our-oauth-engine">Planning the upgrade to our OAuth engine</h2>
      <a href="#planning-the-upgrade-to-our-oauth-engine" aria-hidden="true" class="relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5">
        <svg width="16" height="16" viewBox="0 0 24 24"><path fill="currentcolor" d="m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z"></path></svg>
      </a>
    </div>
    <p>Years ago, we deployed <a href="https://github.com/ory/hydra" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"><u>Hydra</u></a>, an open-source OAuth engine, to power Cloudflare OAuth under the hood. That deployment served us well when usage was limited, but as the developer platform grew and agentic workflows became more common, it became clear that we needed a major upgrade to unlock new capabilities and improve performance.Â </p><p>As we planned the upgrade, we decided to do two smaller sequential upgrades rather than doing one large upgrade.Â  First, we would move to the latest 1.X release, evaluate any behavior or performance changes, and then proceed with the 2.X upgrade.</p><p>During our upgrade planning, it became clear that even the 1.X upgrade would<i> </i>still impact customers because the Hydra database required extensive schema migrations that:</p><ol><li><p>Created indexes in a manner that would claim an exclusive lock on critical tables, preventing active users from performing important OAuth operationsÂ </p></li><li><p>Added columns to critical tables, and moved other columns to new tables</p></li></ol><p>There was also a quirk in the version of Hydra we were using in which the SDK would perform SELECT * operations, causing deserialization issues with the schema changes.</p><p>To prevent user impact, we rewrote the SQL migrations to use features such as CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY, and built a custom version of Hydra which selected explicit columns rather than SELECT *.</p><p>With the latest 1.X upgrade planned out, we now needed to create a plan for the even larger 2.X upgrade. We identified three potential options, and weighed the benefits and drawbacks of each one. Doing an in-place upgrade was not going to work for us, due to the sheer amount of schema changes the major version bump brought with it. We decided that a blue-green strategy would work, but there was more that needed to be done than simply flipping a switch to start using the new version. The upgrade and migration process would take multiple hours, and we needed the system to continue functioning correctly in that time window.</p><p>The first blue-green option would involve disabling writes to the database, preventing any new authorizations from occurring. This means they would not be lost in the transition, but it also meant that nobody would be able to use existing OAuth apps unless they already had a valid credential. It also presented another large problem: if users needed to revoke access from an application for any reason, it would not be possible while the upgrade was being performed.</p><p>To combat these issues, we came up with a way to leave writes to the database enabled, at the cost of losing some of them in the switch to the green version. The first thing to solve was minimizing the number of writes for new tokens. There was an operational lever we pulled: increasing the expiry time of tokens to multiple hours. This would allow apps that received new tokens before the upgrade to continue using them without needing to refresh.</p><p>With reducing writes solved, we needed to come up with a way to not lose any revocations our users performed during the upgrade window. To do this, we created a queue system (using <a href="https://developers.cloudflare.com/queues/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"><u>Cloudflare Queues</u></a>!) which, after a revocation event, would have a record written into the queue with information about that revocation. This would allow us to drain the queue with the database flipped to the green version, replaying all revocation events that took place in the time window in which they would have been lost. This was critical to get right, otherwise applications that users had revoked would inadvertently have their access restored.</p>
    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h2 id="executing-the-upgrade">Executing the upgrade</h2>
      <a href="#executing-the-upgrade" aria-hidden="true" class="relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5">
        <svg width="16" height="16" viewBox="0 0 24 24"><path fill="currentcolor" d="m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z"></path></svg>
      </a>
    </div>
    
    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h3 id="upgrading-to-1-x">Upgrading to 1.X</h3>
      <a href="#upgrading-to-1-x" aria-hidden="true" class="relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5">
        <svg width="16" height="16" viewBox="0 0 24 24"><path fill="currentcolor" d="m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z"></path></svg>
      </a>
    </div>
    <p>From an operational point of view, our first upgrade to the last 1.X release went off without any hitches. Our custom database migrations ran faster than we expected, with no user impact. We had to do a hard cutover to the new version because the old version was unable to introspect tokens that were created by the newer version.</p><p>After the cutover, we saw an increase in refresh token errors that we had not seen before. This ended up being due to stricter refresh invalidation behaviors in the new version; if a refresh token was reused, Hydra would invalidate the whole access and refresh token chain. This is problematic for Wrangler and MCP clients. These clients both have a high request volume, and a single reused refresh token would invalidate the entire session.</p><p>We mitigated this by adding refresh token coalescing behavior to our Worker which routes OAuth traffic to the correct destination. This allowed us to briefly cache the refresh token request before it reached Hydra, so that if we detected a retry we could short-circuit the request and respond without invalidating the tokens. Fortunately, 2.X versions of Hydra have a configurable ârefresh token grace periodâ, which resolves this by allowing a refresh token to be retried for a period of time without invalidating the whole chain.</p>
    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h3 id="upgrading-to-2-x">Upgrading to 2.X</h3>
      <a href="#upgrading-to-2-x" aria-hidden="true" class="relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5">
        <svg width="16" height="16" viewBox="0 0 24 24"><path fill="currentcolor" d="m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z"></path></svg>
      </a>
    </div>
    <p>Since multiple hours of high user-facing impact would not be acceptable, we had our blue-green upgrade strategy set. At a high level, this sounds simple; the migrations would run on a copy of our production database, and then cut over along with the new Hydra version after they complete. In reality, there were a <i>lot </i>more moving parts:</p><ul><li><p>Enable revocation replay capture queue</p></li><li><p>Copy and restore our database to the new target</p></li><li><p>Targeted data cleanup â existing data violated some new constraints introduced in the newer versions, which could prevent migrations from succeeding</p></li><li><p>Perform cutovers on the Hydra service along with two additional critical internal systems simultaneously to prevent any errors</p></li><li><p>Post-cutover monitoring and validation</p></li></ul>
          <figure class="kg-card kg-image-card">
          <Image src="https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/tbiQb2wX9zyyC2n6cOMmv/706bacb728d5abef09117c3893ec288d/hydra_upgrade_diagram.png" alt="" class="kg-image" width="819" height="692" loading="lazy"/>
          </figure><p>We chose an upgrade window when Hydra had the lowest request volume per second to minimize lost token writes. Other than some timeout tuning, our production migrations ran well against the new database: the net runtime in production was approximately three hours. After the migrations completed, we carefully rolled out the new version of the Hydra service, along with two additional system configs to flip our systems to use the new SDK version.</p><p>Shortly after cutting traffic over, we observed that a data cleanup job in our authorization service (which relies on the Hydra consent session API) was being overeager in its purging of OAuth policy data. After investigation, we discovered that there was an issue in one of the Hydra migrations that corrupted the state of certain valid OAuth sessions, which resulted in the migration marking them as invalid. The valid sessions being corrupted caused a disagreement between Hydra and our authorization service, manifesting as an increase in 403s. To mitigate this, we did data restorations and began work on improvements for OAuth authorization behaviors to remove reliance on static policy data.</p><p>Beyond the data cleanup issue, there were some additional small fixes more driven by specific client behaviors which we landed quickly.Â </p><p>With the Hydra version upgrade complete, OAuth traffic has remained stable with improved system performance and reliability for our customers. It also brought production onto the same foundation our newer OAuth APIs had already been validated against in staging, clearing the way for our <a href="https://developers.cloudflare.com/changelog/post/2026-06-03-public-oauth-clients/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"><u>self-managed OAuth release on June 3</u></a>.Â </p>
    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h2 id="performance-improvements">Performance improvements</h2>
      <a href="#performance-improvements" aria-hidden="true" class="relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5">
        <svg width="16" height="16" viewBox="0 0 24 24"><path fill="currentcolor" d="m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z"></path></svg>
      </a>
    </div>
    <p>After completing a large upgrade like this, it is always rewarding and illuminating to look at some broad metrics about the impact. We gathered additional metrics during the database migrations, and observed considerable performance improvements after the upgrade was complete.</p>
    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h3 id="database">Database</h3>

    <div class="flex anchor relative">
      <h2 id="self-managed-oauth-for-all">Self-managed OAuth for all</h2>
      <a href="#self-managed-oauth-for-all" aria-hidden="true" class="relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5">
        <svg width="16" height="16" viewBox="0 0 24 24"><path fill="currentcolor" d="m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z"></path></svg>
      </a>
    </div>
    <p>Opening up OAuth to all customers is an important step toward a broader Cloudflare app ecosystem. Today, any Cloudflare customer can create their own OAuth applications and build integrations on top of Cloudflare. Weâre extremely excited to launch Cloudflare self-managed OAuth for all.Â </p><p>To get started, take a look at our <a href="https://developers.cloudflare.com/fundamentals/oauth/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"><u>documentation</u></a> or jump straight to the OAuth apps page in the <a href="https://dash.cloudflare.com/?to=/:account/oauth-clients" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"><u>dashboard</u></a> and create your first OAuth app.</p></div></section><section class="post-full-content flex flex-row flex-wrap mw7 center mb3"><div class="post-content lh-copy w-100 gray1 bt b--gray8 pt3"><span></span></div></section><astro-slot> <!--[if astro]>server-island-start<![endif]--><script type="module" data-astro-rerun data-island-id="eff8b06b-6456-484b-ada2-215ea0cb8f92">const headers = new Headers({});
